## 2026-05-26 · Post · The hardest part of creating a private-audit-like AI Auditor is having a private-audit-like signal-t


Tags (automatic): AI-assisted hunting


### Post — @DevDacian — 2026-05-26 11:25 UTC (2026-05-26 12:25 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/2059234372944073036
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> The hardest part of creating a private-audit-like AI Auditor is having a private-audit-like signal-to-noise ratio.
>
> 1. Private audits need to provide wide, broad & deep vulnerability coverage - they need to catch lots of things
>
> 2. At the same time they also need to have very high signal-to-noise ratio with minimal or no false positives & inflated severities
>
> Solace in its current form is very good at 1, finding a wide range of valuable issues in never-before-seen code we are auditing.
>
> But its signal-to-noise ratio averages around 50-50 with +/-20 variance skew in both directions.
>
> When using it internally human auditors verify its findings and edit/adjust as required prior to presenting to the client.
>
> But when using it directly the clients need to do this filtering/review themselves with makes for a worse user experience.
>
> This is something that is actively being worked on - new Solace releases are getting major improvements aimed at increasing the signal-to-noise ratio.
>
> The trickiest part is doing this without losing significant valid findings but I have some great ideas & strategies which should help overcome this risk to a significant degree.

## 2026-03-30 · Post · Q) How are you using 5-10m tokens per day?


Tags (automatic): Smart contract / DeFi, AI-assisted hunting


### Post — @DevDacian — 2026-03-30 10:47 UTC (2026-03-30 11:47 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/2038568883884572930
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> Q) How are you using 5-10m tokens per day?
>
> 1⃣ I created & train Solace, the latest experimental iteration of our internal vulnerability scanner. Recently I've made some major breakthroughs which unlocked new levels of performance.
>
> Running it can take ~45 mins or more per audit and use 1.5m -> 3m+ tokens depending on the size of the codebase. I have a systematic method for continuing to train it to find new types of vulnerabilities when it misses them.
>
> Doing a few runs each day can easily hit 5-10m tokens.
>
> 2⃣ I created & train Bastion, which to my knowledge is the first Specialist AI capable of automated formal verification for Medium complexity smart contracts. I've been quietly using it to ship FV suites as an additional deliverable for my private audit clients.
>
> Running it can take anywhere from 30 mins -> 5 hours+, especially if it struggles which it does on more complicated codebases. Can easily use 500k-1m+ tokens. It doesn't fully replace humans but is great for 3-5 day audits where human FV isn't feasible & can also be an efficiency gain for longer audits where a human does FV using Bastion's output as the starting point.
>
> 3⃣ I created process-reports which automates a lot of our various report workflows, not a great token user but a nice efficiency gain for some of our more complicated report workflows and also minimizes human error.
>
> 4⃣ I created another internal AI that does a lot of my management job & handles a lot of the "business intelligence/optimization" work. Again not a huge token user here but a huge efficiency gainer as it frees up my time and provides very useful business intelligence to enable data-driven decision making.
>
> 5⃣ I'm continually improving these AIs and most likely I will create more specialist AIs whenever I have a need. My goal for 2026 is to automate & optimize away as much of my job as possible, to continue making @cyfrin among the most efficient security providers maximizing value for our clients' spend.
>
> You may think that is foolish, what about your job security etc? Well firstly I do this primarily for fun though the $ is also nice. And secondly I think the future is one of limitless possibilities so if I manage to automate & optimize away most of my job, that will free me to do even more interesting things - and I'll be even better at creating & training specialist AIs so even better equipped for the future.

## 2026-03-20 · Post · 💡Subtle Oracle / Math Bug💡


### Post — @DevDacian — 2026-03-20 12:37 UTC (2026-03-20 13:37 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/2034972532076683487
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> 💡Subtle Oracle / Math Bug💡
>
> When a price aggregator uses multiple feeds to calculate on-going Moving Average (MA), it shouldn't also use as input previous MA values to avoid the MA increasingly reflecting itself rather than the market data.
>
> Using previous values as input is useful to calculate Exponential Moving Average (EMA) where each new observation is blended with previous EMA using a "smoothing factor", causing recent values to have more weight while older values decay exponentially.
>
> The correct way to implement both:
> 1⃣ aggregate multiple raw feeds into one clean price per desired time interval
> 2⃣ run either MA or EMA on that single price series

> The only way to join Cyfrin full-time is to first work as an Eagle contractor & provide great security output on our private audits.

> The only way to get onto a Cyfrin private audit as an Eagle contractor is to:

## 2026-02-03 · Post · Q) Differences between working at audit firm vs protocol?


### Post — @DevDacian — 2026-02-03 11:43 UTC (2026-02-03 12:43 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/2018651631647334595
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> Q) Differences between working at audit firm vs protocol?
>
> 1⃣ Variety - at an audit firm you'll be auditing a wide range of different protocols, typically going from one audit to the next. There will be repeat clients that you audit incrementally but also many different new clients.
>
> Working at a protocol you'll get to know that one protocol really well; you'll get to contribute to its design from a security focus, contribute test suites, formal verification specs, perform internal reviews and handle bug bounty reports.
>
> Larger protocols will have multiple components but ultimately there will be less variety since you'll be focusing on one protocol.
>
> 2⃣ Pace - at a very busy & efficient audit firm the pace can be quite hectic continuously going from one audit to another with only occasional "research" downtime.
>
> Working at a protocol the pace may be more relaxed doing a mix of auditing, test/FV writing, architecture/defensive recommendations, bug bounty submission reviews etc for the same client.
>
> 3⃣ Team - some audit firms will frequently bring in specialist contractors or at least mix up the internal teams, creating unique team selections specialized to each audit. Many auditors really like this as it has them frequently working with other different top auditors so they learn new ways of thinking, iron sharpening iron!
>
> Working at a protocol you'll mostly be working with the same internal team; there will occasionally be some interaction with external auditors, bug bounty hunters etc but for the most part you'll be working with the same people and many of them may be non-auditors such as devs.
>
> 4⃣ Comp - protocols will typically want you to take a significant portion of your comp in tokens, potentially with long lock-up / vesting periods. You should carefully consider:
>
> * is the protocol actually profitable, being able to sustain itself from fees or generated income? If yes this is favorable, if not this is a warning sign
>
> * does the protocol's token price chart look "down & to the right" - price mostly just going down over time? If yes this is a warning sign as it indicates the protocol is likely not profitable and dependent on dumping its token
>
> * how old is the protocol, what does the vesting period look like for the founders, VCs, early hires etc? By the time your tokens vest will they have dumped their huge bags driving the price massively down?
>
> * what is the protocol's current fully diluted market cap, is it really inflated for its current revenues, transaction volume etc? Can it realistically grow by 10x or more from here? Many VC-backed protocols TGE at huge valuations while not being profitable and depend on dumping their tokens, sending price and market cap "down & to the right" over time
>
> * does the protocol have a buy-n-burn mechanism where it uses a significant percentage of the profits generated by the protocol (if it is profitable) to consistently buy-n-burn its tokens?
>
> * does the protocol have an infinite mint where the protocol can continually decide to issue new tokens?
>
> * if you were an investor thinking about buying the token, why should the protocol's token price go "up & to the right" especially when there are likely large vesting cliffs to be unlocked by founders, early VCs etc that will be massively dumping before your tokens unlock?
>
> This is something I'd be really careful about, if accepting tokens as a significant portion of comp I'd heavily discount the actual value you are likely to realize from the tokens. Protocols may try to sell you on a huge dollar figure comp "package" but you need to find out what % of that is in tokens then heavily discount that component, don't believe the big number unless it is straight cash.
>
> 5⃣ Job Security - the reality is many protocols in the second-half of 2025 laid off tons of staff including some security-related staff.
>
> Many protocols are not profitable, they are dependent on VC funding and endlessly dumping their tokens to say alive. Be careful accepting a role with such a protocol because you may be out in the next round of lay-offs. Many protocols will see you as a "cost-center"  which is generally a negative light to be seen in.
>
> Alternatively if a protocol is profitable, is able to sustain itself from generated income and has no need to continually dump its token, then that is a much safer bet.
>
> In contrast busy audit firms will see you as a "profit-center" if you are efficiently producing great security output with high utilization so you are unlikely to be fired if the firm is growing and able to secure sufficient work.
>
> Audit firms are typically profitable businesses or they wouldn't continue to exist so you'll generally have better job security joining a top firm as long as you perform well.

## 2026-01-13 · Post · 💡AI's Transformative Impact on Web3 Security: Predictions from Late 2026 Onwards💡


Tags (automatic): Smart contract / DeFi, Blockchain / L1-L2 / bridge, Memory corruption / native, AI-assisted hunting


### Post — @DevDacian — 2026-01-13 12:19 UTC (2026-01-13 13:19 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/2011050364758278282
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> 💡AI's Transformative Impact on Web3 Security: Predictions from Late 2026 Onwards💡
>
> From late 2026 onwards what was once a domain dominated by human auditors poring over code will evolve into a hybrid ecosystem where AI amplifies human expertise, reduces costs, and reshapes incentives.
>
> 1⃣ Lowering Total Cost of Securing a Protocol
>
> One of the most tangible impacts of AI will be a reduction in the overall cost of securing web3 protocols. Today in early 2026 we're already seeing specialist AI agents detecting a broad spectrum of vulnerabilities. Foundational models continue to improve in reasoning and skilled researchers are layering on custom heuristics, creating specialist AIs that can find valuable bugs "at scale".
>
> External audits which can easily run into six figures for a comprehensive review have long been a pain point for protocols. If an initial audit uncovers too many findings protocols often face costly re-audits, delaying launches and inflating budgets. By late 2026 specialist "PR Review" agents will be commonly used during development to catch bugs early - and some specialist AI products can catch an impressive range of bugs today; this will only improve by late 2026 & onwards.
>
> AI will also supercharge testing methodologies, making it much easier and cost-effective for developers to commonly deliver hardened codebases featuring invariant testing and formal verification.
>
> The net effect? Protocols will require fewer external audits, lowering the total cost of securing a protocol.
>
> 2⃣ The Evolving Role of Human Auditors
>
> Human auditors won't disappear; instead, their work will pivot toward higher-value activities. By late 2026, a growing number of auditors will shift from manual code reviews to developing and refining heuristics for specialist AIs.
>
> The trend of auditors consolidating into larger firms will continue; expectations for efficiency will increase across the board. The best auditors will leverage AI to become super-human, highly-efficient bug-finding machines.
>
> 3⃣ Capped Auditor Remuneration in Maturing Market
>
> The web3 security space is maturing rapidly. In the Wild West days of 2021-2024, sky-high fees and long waiting times were justified by rampant hacks and scarce expertise. But as many skilled auditors transition into creating heuristics for Specialist AI auditors vastly improving their vulnerability detection, clients will increasingly prioritize speed & value.
>
> Market share will gravitate toward quality brands that consistently deliver great security output at an efficient price point, who provide great value for money. Intermediate-Senior human auditors will still be able to make a good living but the extremely expensive ones may struggle to remain consistently booked, a trend which played out especially through the first half of 2025 and will accelerate in the future.
>
> New & Junior auditors will struggle as the bar to achieving professional success will be raised higher & there will be more competition than ever; they will be completely replaced by Specialist AIs who will consistently perform better providing more value than them.
>
> 4⃣ Shorter Exploit Windows and the Elimination of Common Bugs
>
> AI's double-edged sword will manifest in exploit dynamics. On the dark side, blackhat AIs will exploit vulnerabilities faster than ever. A protocol launching with bugs in late 2026 could be drained in hours, not days, as blackhat AIs trawl the blockchain in real-time.
>
> Conversely, mass adoption of specialist AIs by developers will nearly eliminate certain bug classes. Reentrancy, integer overflows, incorrect rounding direction and access control flaws could become relics, caught routinely during the development process and not even making it to external audits let alone to live deployment.
>
> 5⃣ Human-AI Symbiosis
>
> Here's the key: Specialist AIs won't stay effective without human input. Auditors and researchers must continually update them with the latest heuristics, drawing from private/competitive audits, post-mortems of hacks and other threat data. This creates a virtuous cycle—humans teach AIs, AIs protect protocols, and the ecosystem grows more resilient providing increasingly efficient security "at scale".
>
> In summary, from late 2026 onwards, AI won't replace web3 security professionals but will redefine the field as a collaborative powerhouse. Roles will evolve & markets will mature - for devs, auditors, and protocols: Embrace AI now, or risk being left behind.

>
> 2) created measurement/tracking system to objectively measure auditor relative performance & track lots of other stats related to the audit business, also to incentivize both individual achievement & team collaboration

> 3) using 2, identified two hidden inefficiencies in the audit business processes, then designed and implemented strategies to minimize them
>

>
> 5) successfully branched out into Solana/Rust, Move/Sui/Aptos, web2sec/off-chain & Formal Verification audits
>

> 7) consistent marketing on twitter posting almost every business day

## 2025-12-10 · Post · Useful heuristic from recent Balancer hack:


### Post — @DevDacian — 2025-12-10 10:30 UTC (2025-12-10 11:30 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1998701725944070440
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> Useful heuristic from recent Balancer hack:
> * function X has one rounding direction
> * X is called by multiple parent functions
> * is X's rounding direction correct for all parent callers?
>
> Easy, effective &amp; perfect for AIs to automatically identify incorrect rounding.

> 7⃣ Give token allocations to key KOLs so they shill it to retail
>
> 8⃣ Time token release to bull market all-time high BTC/ETH prices
>
> 9⃣ Exit via TGE dumping your founder/team tokens on retail and/or via OTC deals

> You are now a successfully exited founder - congrats! Setup a VC shop and run the same game many times over with whatever the next hot things are.

## 2025-10-20 · Post · Shout out @MrPotatoMagic & myself great result on this challenging cross-chain TradFi auditing findi


Tags (automatic): Smart contract / DeFi


### Post — @DevDacian — 2025-10-20 08:58 UTC (2025-10-20 09:58 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1980196955176489257
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> Shout out @MrPotatoMagic & myself great result on this challenging cross-chain TradFi auditing finding:
>
> * 4 Med
> * 4 Low
> * 15 Info & 9 Gas
>
> This audit was quite challenging because we were auditing a TradFi protocol's Solidity integration between Wormhole & Circle CCTPv2, at a time when:
>
> * Wormhole's official documentation and Solidity code examples were all related to CCTPv1 integration
> * Wormhole's publicly available off-chain code only integrated with CCTPv1
>
> So based on the publicly available information at the time, it was extremely difficult to verify the correctness of the proposed implementation - we couldn't see how the Solidity Wormhole <-> CCTPv2 integration should look nor the off-chain code that would process the emitted events then call the CCTPv2 API.
>
> We overcame these limitations by:
> * reaching out to our contacts to get some draft unpublished integration specs
> * simplified the client's protocol while keeping the same core integration logic, wrote some Foundry scripts to deploy & test our simplified contracts then successfully performed live end-to-end integration testing
>
> In the end we were able to find some nice edge-case bugs delivering good value to the client under challenging circumstances!

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/G3sR_JXXYAA1B1X.png — no alt text provided by author

## 2025-10-09 · Post · Q) When I work with Cyfrin I get paid straight away after an audit, but when working with firms X/Y/


Tags (automatic): Smart contract / DeFi


### Post — @DevDacian — 2025-10-09 02:12 UTC (2025-10-09 03:12 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1976108386321301618
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> Q) When I work with Cyfrin I get paid straight away after an audit, but when working with firms X/Y/Z it takes weeks and even months to get paid. Why is that?
>
> Being asked this a lot over the last few months; without knowing the internals of firms X/Y/Z, there are a number of reasons why firms would delay paying their bills especially to auditors:
>
> 1) Earning interest / yield - by delaying payment, a firm can park that capital in an interest-earning account or a yield-generating DeFi strategy, earning yield on the $ that is owed to auditors for as long as they can delay payment.
>
> The firm profits at the expense of auditors who could have done the same if they had been paid promptly.
>
> 2) Zero Cost Financing - the firm does not have enough work and/or is not getting paid in a timely manner by their clients, and/or has poor cash flow management.
>
> Delaying payment to their auditors is effectively an "interest-free loan", a zero-cost financing which helps them maintain adequate cash flow to service more important business expenses.
>
> Since the firm is giving the auditors work there is a power imbalance here as if the auditors complain about late payment the firm may stop giving them work.
>
> 3) Trading while insolvent - the firm is secretly insolvent but continuing to trade (illegal in many countries) hoping that its financial position will improve.
>
> If the firm is unable to improve its financial position, unpaid suppliers may never be paid if the firm declares bankruptcy going into liquidation.
>
> Ultimately this is a free market; auditors are free to engage or not engage with any firm. If auditors don't like waiting weeks or months to get paid, then simply don't engage with such firms.

### Post — @DevDacian — 2025-09-23 09:45 UTC (2025-09-23 10:45 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1970424189300756597
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> Five types of people I have become wary of in the web3sec community:
>
> 1⃣ people who frequently publicly sling mud at others, trying to raise themselves up by putting others down. This is really low-class, limited-mindset, schoolyard-level behavior
>
> 2⃣ people who misrepresent their relationships with others to try and get clients, win deals etc. This is deceitful behavior to gain material advantage and larger brands need to be especially aware of this
>
> 3⃣ people who never say anything good about another brand, product or service, but they only say good things about their own brand and won't ever praise or recognize good work by others especially competitors
>
> 4⃣ people with a savior/god complex who think they are the anointed ones, the chosen protagonists, our saviors the only true heroes, and everyone else sucks in comparison to them
>
> 5⃣ people who when you ask them a logical question, they don't respond to the question you asked but instead launch into a personal or ad-hominem attack

## 2025-06-13 · Post · Over 2 days this week I successfully vibe coded 2 very useful tools to help our sales and marketing 


Tags (automatic): AI-assisted hunting


### Post — @DevDacian — 2025-06-13 00:16 UTC (2025-06-13 01:16 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1933317406551941546
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> Over 2 days this week I successfully vibe coded 2 very useful tools to help our sales and marketing teams using @grok !
>
> Being a former software engineer I could have easily done this myself but I wanted to challenge myself and see what AI can do by committing to not writing any code, but that all code should come from the AI!
>
> Project #1 - 2 Models For Quoting Audits (Spreadsheet)
>
> Using Grok I created two models for quoting audits where the sales team can easily input a range of potential audit parameters and get a range of prices.
>
> These models are really cool because they completely eliminate human error of quoting too low or too high, and force the sales team to think in terms to the trade-offs which occur with different audit parameters and pricing levels.
>
> The models also make it very easy to quickly offer multiple tiers of service, to find the perfect fit for the client's budget! It has already supercharged our quoting ability which should be much more efficient and consistent!
>
> This task was not that complicated and @grok handled it efficiently & effortlessly with no significant bugs, bravo!
>
> Project #2 - Public Audit Repo Automatic Data Table Generation (Python)
>
> Our public audit repo previously had 1 main table where each audit appears in sequential order and has some metadata such as:
> * type of "Tech" involved
> * Crit/High/Med/Low/Info/Gas findings for each audit
> * a "Totals" row summarizing everything
>
> When making an audit report public, auditors would have to add a new row to that table then manually update the "Totals" row which sometimes result in human errors where fields were incorrectly updated or not updated at all.
>
> The first step which @grok easily handled was to update the "Totals" column when auditors added a new row.
>
> Then I started adding more advanced features to automagically generate additional tables by "Tech" type every time a new row is added and to calculate "Total" and "Average" stats for each "Tech" type. This is very useful for Sales and Marketing to quickly showcase our impressive results to potential clients of similar audits we've done to their own protocols!
>
> As Grok was implementing the additional "Tech" tables generation, I found a lot of bugs testing with various inputs and scenarios which Grok didn't think to handle by itself, and I had to work iteratively with Grok to get it to resolve the bugs one by one.
>
> It felt like working with a very junior developer where it would write code without thinking about sometimes quite obvious edge-cases that needed to be handled!
>
> Eventually there were only 3 bugs left, and Grok started adding debug outputs as it was having difficulty fixing them. By incrementally adding debug outputs it eventually solved 2 of the 3 bugs!
>
> There was only 1 really nasty bug left, which it took Grok a few hours to solve by trying many different things and adding lots more debug outputs.
>
> I eventually started to give Grok strategies & tips it could use to prevent the bug which helped it a lot - if I hadn't done this it may not have been able to solve the bug, as it seemed to stop making much progress and actually regress at times.
>
> This shows that a seasoned programmer or software engineer will have a much better time using AI than a non-coder, as they can instruct the AI on strategies to use when stuck on a bug that a non-programmer and the AI itself won't think or know about.
>
> Finally @grok nailed that last bug and the resulting code worked extremely well and was very robust, perfectly handling all the edge cases and previous bugs I had identified!
>
> Ultimately I'm very happy with my @grok AI vibe coding experience and was happy to produce some useful tools to help and supercharge my teammates in Sales & Marketing!

## 2025-06-09 · Post · Very short private audits (1-3 days) should generally be avoided if they feature:


### Post — @DevDacian — 2025-06-09 10:30 UTC (2025-06-09 11:30 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1932022361844293883
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> Very short private audits (1-3 days) should generally be avoided if they feature:
> * significant external integrations
> * layers of complexity
> * interactions with out-of-scope custom contracts
> * poor / non-existent test suite
>
> These private audits don't provide a good risk:reward ratio as they are:
> * high risk (very possible to miss critical issues in the very short time frame)
> * low reward (small money due to small number of audit days)
>
> Very short private audits can work well for small codebases with:
> * no significant external integrations
> * not many layers of complexity
> * no interactions with out-of-scope custom contracts
> * good / great existing test suite

## 2025-05-22 · Post · My personal opinion is that most if not all current AI Auditor services are some form of grift or sc


Tags (automatic): Smart contract / DeFi, AI-assisted hunting


### Post — @DevDacian — 2025-05-22 10:30 UTC (2025-05-22 11:30 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1925499380968431765
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> My personal opinion is that most if not all current AI Auditor services are some form of grift or scam, often accompanied with very deceptive marketing about amazing findings that were or would be duped to hell in public audit contests.
>
> The only way to change my mind is for AI Auditor services to:
>
> 1) regularly find 80-90% of what a junior auditor can find, this way codebases can get efficient low-cost QA done prior to real audits. Such AI Auditor services should explicitly note that their services are QA/preparatory work prior to real audits; that they do not replace nor are equivalent to real audits
>
> 2) if they truly have some "intelligence", find the same (or ideally more) as the current "dumb" static analyzers but with drastically lower false positive rates. Even if they just wrap the static analyzers but use their "intelligence" to accurately filter out false positives, this would be a useful improvement
>
> 3) (holy grail) regularly find high-impact findings that no or few other auditors can find in public contests or bug bounties
>
> Until one of these 3 criteria is achieved, AI Auditors are mostly useless and should not be relied upon for security by any serious protocols, regardless of what their marketing might claim. The security pipeline for serious protocols remains the same: multiple audits by top firms, public competitive audit & bug bounty post deployment.
>
> That being said, the current AI Auditor services do have one valid use case - to enrich their teams and investors. AI Auditor grifts & scams are nothing new and have been very profitable for the people who run them; I present to you the chart of the OG AI Auditor from Jan 2023: "0x0 AI Smart Contract Auditor".
>
> If you had invested $10,000 into the OG AI Auditor at the mid-range price in the first 5 weeks, today you'd have ~$1,600,000 and at the peak ~$9,000,000! Yet where are all the amazing audit contest wins, epic bug bounties received & stacked private audit reports of this AI Auditor over the last ~2.5 years? Nowhere to be found!🤣🤣🤣
>
> So just because most if not all current AI Auditor services are scams and grifts, doesn't mean they won't be massively profitable especially for their early investors.
>
> And it also doesn't mean that in the future an actually useful AI Auditor service won't emerge; I do believe 1) and 2) will be achieved in the future which will have an overall positive impact on the security outcomes of our industry.
>
> If you are actively developing an AI Auditor service, my challenge to you is to develop 1) and 2) then conclusively prove it to the world - you'll make a ton of $ in a legitimate manner and genuinely help our industry.
>
> But please don't pretend with your marketing that the AI "Auditor" service you offer provides the same or better security outcomes than the existing security pipeline used by top protocols consisting of multiple audits by top firms + public competitive audit, because it doesn't.
>
> Your AI isn't catching what multiple top firms and competitive audits miss; if it was you'd have millions of dollars not from investor funding but from a fat stack of big $ contest wins & massive $ bug bounties.

## 2025-05-20 · Post · Our goal on every @cyfrin private audit is to maximize value for every client. This starts at the be


### Post — @DevDacian — 2025-05-20 11:52 UTC (2025-05-20 12:52 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1924795337195323665
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> Our goal on every @cyfrin private audit is to maximize value for every client. This starts at the beginning with auditor selection where I hand-pick the best-suited auditors for the needs of each particular client from our full-time staff and large pool of specialist "Eagle" contractors.
>
> In auditor selection I'm looking for auditors with proven skill & experience in the particular niche a client requires. Our auditors frequently have research weeks prior to taking on new clients, enabling them to go into audits prepared and ready to go!
>
> During the audit itself we are in frequent contact with the client; on longer audits at the end of each week we deliver a draft report with our findings so far. Clients love that we are in communication and can see that the audit is progressing, questions are being asked & answered, findings are being found.
>
> But we go beyond only findings and a professional report; our auditors deliver additional value by:
>
> 1⃣ Permissions model hardening - when auditing permissioned systems, we examine the purpose and scope of each permissioned entity seeking to harden the permissions model as much as possible
>
> 2⃣ Threat model analysis - we put ourselves in the shoes of various entities in the protocol, viewing the protocol through their eyes to see how each actor can potentially cause damage
>
> 3⃣ Protocol architecture analysis & advice - we study how the protocol currently fits together, and how that could be improved to increase the efficiency, readability and maintainability of the code
>
> 4⃣ Defining protocol & contract invariants, especially low-gas invariants which can be implement directly into the code - often we find ways to break the invariants we define, as defining them gives us valuable targets to aim for
>
> 5⃣ Test suite creation & enhancement - we often write tests including fuzzers to explore scenarios seeing how the protocol behaves in different states. These exploratory tests lead to bugs but the tests themselves have value and are delivered to clients as an additional extra
>
> 6⃣ Gas optimization - in seeking to understand the role of every single variable in the protocol, we frequently find variables that are redundant, identical storage reads. Sometimes in seeking to understand a function we'll re-write it to simplify it which also results in a more efficient implementation that goes into the report
>
> Our ultimate goal in every audit is to do everything possible to maximize value to the client; to leave no stone unturned, no path unexplored, doing our best every time for every client!

> 💡 The @YieldoorFi Gas Optimizoor 💡
>
> 9 Tips plus full deep dive showing how I reduced:
> * overall gas costs by 15.43%
> * liquidation function gas costs by 13.21% -&gt; 16.91%
>
> Plus how gas optimization could have netted you 2 High findings in Yieldoor's recent audit contest 👇


### Thread part 2 — @DevDacian — 2025-03-17 10:39 UTC (2025-03-17 11:39 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1901584169425342696
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 559, like 8, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 1⃣ Measuring Gas Costs
>
> Foundry has at least 3 in-built ways to measure gas costs:
>
> 1) reports via `forge test --gas-report`
> 2) snapshots via `forge snapshot`
> 3) gas section snapshots via cheatcodes to measure gas costs of arbitrary code segments inside unit tests 👇


### Thread part 3 — @DevDacian — 2025-03-17 10:39 UTC (2025-03-17 11:39 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1901584172663337167
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 533, like 5, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 2⃣ Cache Identical In-Function Storage Reads
>
> Reading identical values multiple times from storage is expensive!
>
> Look inside functions for identical storage reads then simply cache them once and read from the cache to get a nice gas cost reduction!👇 [pic.x.com/gsd8L8y6ny]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GmPBWbGboAAW0bz.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 4 — @DevDacian — 2025-03-17 10:39 UTC (2025-03-17 11:39 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1901584176106889672
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 476, like 4, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 3⃣ Bypass Stack Too Deep
>
> Having trouble caching inside a local variable within a function due to "stack too deep" errors?
>
> Define a struct to hold the cached fields and a function to retrieve them 👇 [pic.x.com/DoPvsNSiav]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GmPAmmIacAARBoZ.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 5 — @DevDacian — 2025-03-17 10:39 UTC (2025-03-17 11:39 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1901584179571343844
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 444, like 3, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 4⃣ Hidden Identical Storage Reads In Modifiers
>
> When optimizing a function, check the storage reads the function does compared to the storage reads done by its modifiers.
>
> Often you'll find modifiers which perform lots of unnecessary identical storage reads! 👇 [pic.x.com/x18CaVhTlL]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GmPB3gkaEAAqQZC.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 6 — @DevDacian — 2025-03-17 10:39 UTC (2025-03-17 11:39 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1901584182742228997
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 410, like 3, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 5⃣ Hidden Identical Storage Reads In Child Functions
>
> Take a look at the child functions your main functions calls; are they reading the same storage slots again?
>
> Look especially for child functions taking ids (which have already been loaded) as input parameters! 👇 [pic.x.com/5p4OmNbDtr]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GmPCkJJaMAAoV90.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 7 — @DevDacian — 2025-03-17 10:39 UTC (2025-03-17 11:39 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1901584185988612308
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 415, like 3, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 6⃣ Copy All, Use Few
>
> Look for code which copies a struct from storage -&gt; memory, but only uses a small number of that struct's storage slots resulting in multiple unnecessary storage reads! 👇 [pic.x.com/onCPzeFtme]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GmPDTDtaIAAjuli.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 8 — @DevDacian — 2025-03-17 10:39 UTC (2025-03-17 11:39 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1901584189553861103
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 407, like 3, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 7⃣ Fail Fast Prior To Storage Reads
>
> Fail fast by checking input-related error conditions first - only start reading storage once all input validation (which doesn't need storage data) has successfully passed! 👇 [pic.x.com/0dMGGPvXiK]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GmPD6C8aAAA9OGm.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 9 — @DevDacian — 2025-03-17 10:39 UTC (2025-03-17 11:39 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1901584192447852805
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 467, like 5, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 0

> 8⃣ High #1 - Use Immutable When Only Set In Constructor
>
> For non-upgradeable contracts, slots only set in constructor should be declared `immutable`.
>
> Using this optimization you'd have found `Leverager::feeRecipient` was never set so fees either lost or liquidation reverts depending on token behavior! 👇


### Thread part 10 — @DevDacian — 2025-03-17 10:39 UTC (2025-03-17 11:39 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1901584196281540903
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 803, like 6, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 9⃣ High #2 - Incorrect Storage Slot Read
>
> Looking for identical storage slot reads, you'd have noticed `collectFees` was incorrectly re-reading `mainPosition.tickUpper` likely due to a copy + paste error! 👇 [pic.x.com/hnK2sdEQgZ]

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 129, impression 5853, like 128, quote 1, reply 5, retweet 20

> Mismatched precision bugs occur in large smart contract protocols when different contracts modify the same common storage slots using different precision.
>
> These bugs are usually Critical/High leading to loss of funds or denial of service. Find them using this easy guide 👇


### Thread part 2 — @DevDacian — 2025-01-16 09:26 UTC (2025-01-16 10:26 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1879822543961620986
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 4, impression 440, like 7, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 1) Install ripgrep (https://t.co/XUmNniHlBq) to perform recursive greps
>
> On OSX this is as easy as `brew install rg`


### Thread part 3 — @DevDacian — 2025-01-16 09:26 UTC (2025-01-16 10:26 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1879822547044462837
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 2, impression 486, like 6, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 2) Use ripgrep to search the source directory for every line of code that modifies a particular storage slot.
>
> In this example we find every place where `depositedUsdc` is modified:
>
> $ rg "depositedUsdc" https://t.co/hheRAwfAYM

## 2025-01-13 · Thread · To find "No Precision Scaling" bugs:


### Post — @DevDacian — 2025-01-13 12:17 UTC (2025-01-13 13:17 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1878778371888615866
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 93, impression 3656, like 99, quote 0, reply 3, retweet 13

> To find "No Precision Scaling" bugs:
>
> 1) annotate each variable with its precision
> 2) look for two variables of different precision added or subtracted together
>
> These bugs are often critical leading to DoS or loss of funds.
>
> Credit to @hansfriese for this effective technique. [pic.x.com/Ou4lXbMZQI]

## 2024-12-09 · Post · Minimum pre-audit checklist:


### Post — @DevDacian — 2024-12-09 10:58 UTC (2024-12-09 11:58 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1866074866203730210
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 30, impression 3180, like 75, quote 0, reply 5, retweet 6

> Minimum pre-audit checklist:
>
> * core functions in working state
> * basic passing tests exercising core functionality
> * stable codebase with fixed commit hash for audit
>
> Optional extras:
>
> * clearly defined function and contract invariants
> * stateless &amp; stateful fuzz tests
> * docs

## 2023-11-08 · Post · Heuristics I'm using to find sick critical smart contract exploits:


Tags (automatic): Smart contract / DeFi


### Post — @DevDacian — 2023-11-08 01:08 UTC (2023-11-08 02:08 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1722058475445109234
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 59, impression 4679, like 63, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 6

> Heuristics I'm using to find sick critical smart contract exploits:
>
> 1) do many small operations result in the same effect as one large operation?
>
> 2) does "total" (stored in its own storage location) equal the sum of all individual user values (stored in a mapping) at all times?

## 2023-10-27 · Post · big complex code = lots of room for attack vectors.


### Post — @DevDacian — 2023-10-27 04:25 UTC (2023-10-27 05:25 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1717759363484672004
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 4, impression 2785, like 29, quote 2, reply 1, retweet 4

> big complex code = lots of room for attack vectors.
>
> but small simple code = likely edge cases haven't been correctly handled -&gt; room for creative attack vectors.
>

## 2023-10-02 · Post · If a project has ~100% test coverage, auditors should check whether the test suite also includes tes


### Post — @DevDacian — 2023-10-02 05:01 UTC (2023-10-02 06:01 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1708708746334249363
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 5, impression 1398, like 16, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 1

> If a project has ~100% test coverage, auditors should check whether the test suite also includes tests using tokens with different precision types?
>
> If the test suite only uses tokens with 18 decimal precision then critical findings will likely exist for non-standard tokens.

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 9, impression 5774, like 29, quote 3, reply 3, retweet 1

> Benefits of PoC:
>
> 1) Finding out your idea didn't actually work,
>
> 2) Learning more about how the system works implementing the PoC leading to better subsequent findings,
>
> 3) Proving the exploit to the judge/client,
>
> 4) PoC easily added to project's automated test suite
>

> Really cool finding from @CodeHawks Stablecoin contest:
>
> * mismatched precision scaling issue for tokens with less than 18 decimal places,
>
> * resulted in wrong fiat_value&lt;-&gt;token_amount calculations,
>
> * weaponized the vuln to steal user collateral during liquidation.
>
> Fantastic! https://twitter.com/hansfriese/status/1691132694099382291

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 23, impression 4413, like 46, quote 0, reply 0, retweet 1

> Niche critical finding ⬇️
>
> When requesting randomness via $LINK, REQUEST_CONFIRMATIONS &gt; depth of common block re-orgs that occur on the target chain(s) the contract is to be deployed on, as block re-orgs change returned randomness!
>

## 2023-05-15 · Post · What is my strategy for PoCs in audits?


### Post — @DevDacian — 2023-05-15 12:12 UTC (2023-05-15 13:12 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1658082886317395968
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 7, impression 1751, like 29, quote 1, reply 2, retweet 2

> What is my strategy for PoCs in audits?
>
> Look for the closest provided test that will match. Goal is to use as much of their setup code as possible, to modify as little as possible.
>
> Take the closest matching test case &amp; copy it into a new test, rename &amp; modify to implement PoC

## 2023-04-10 · Post · Don't care about data returned from call() ? Use low-level assembly call which doesn't automatically

- URL: https://x.com/DevDacian/status/1645356306793123851
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 2, impression 683, like 10, quote 0, reply 0, retweet 0

> Don't care about data returned from call() ? Use low-level assembly call which doesn't automatically copy data returned into memory. More gas-efficient &amp; prevents gas-grieving attacks.
>
